Fibrational approach to Grandis exactness for 2-categories
Elena Caviglia, Zurab Janelidze, Luca Mesiti

Abstract
. In an abelian category, the (bi)fibration of subobjects is isomorphic to the (bi)fibration of quotients. This property captures substantial information about the exactness structure of a category. Indeed, as it was shown by the second author and T. Weighill, categories equipped with a proper factorization system such that the opfibration of subobjects relative to the factorization system is isomorphic to the fibration of relative quotients are precisely the Grandis exact categories. In this paper we characterize those ( 1 , 1 ) (1,1) -proper factorization systems on a 2 2 -category in the sense of M. Dupont and E. Vitale, for which the weak 2 2 -opfibration of relative 2 2 -subobjects is biequivalent to the weak 2-fibration of relative 2 2 -quotients. This results in a new notion of 2 2 -dimensional exactness, which we then compare with similar notions in the context of categories enriched in pointed groupoids arising in the work of M. Dupont and H. Nakaoka.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
18E10, 18E08, 18N10, 18D30

1.
Introduction

One of the central notions in homological algebra is that of an exact sequence. In categorical algebra, there is a wide range of axiomatic frameworks where exact sequences can be defined and studied. At the center of these frameworks is the classical notion of an abelian category, which includes categories of modules over a ring, and in particular, the category of
\mathbb{Z}
-modules (i.e., abelian groups), as well as sheaves of modules.

Extension of homological algebra to
2
-dimensional categories is an on-going endeavor that is in its early phase of development. Motivated by homological properties of symmetric categorical groups (i.e., internal abelian pseudo-groups in the category of categories), a notion of
2
-dimensional abelian category was proposed and studied by M. Dupont
[
4
]
. There is also a related work on
2
-dimensional cohomology theory by H. Nakaoka
[
9
,
10
]
. This paper aims at expanding 2-dimensional homological algebra, with insights from the theories of 2-dimensional fibrations and 2-dimensional factorization systems.

Abelian categories have the following fibrational expression of some of their homological properties: the opfibration of subobjects is isomorphic to the fibration of quotients. In fact, as shown in
[
7
]
, this property is characteristic to Grandis exact categories, which provide an abstract context for capturing good behaviour of exact sequences in abelian categories. In this paper we develop a 2-dimensional analogue of this fibrational approach to exactness, which allows us to introduce a notion of a Grandis exact category in dimension 2. In a forthcoming joint work with Ülo Reimaa
[
6
]
, we prove that the 2-category of
abelian categories (with suitably chosen morphisms) is an example of our notion.

In the pointed case, we obtain a 2-dimensional notion of Puppe exactness that we then compare with similar notions developed by M. Dupont
[
4
]
and H. Nakaoka
[
9
,
10
]
in the context of categories enriched over pointed groupoids. To do so, we introduce a slightly weaker notion of 2-dimensional Puppe exactness that only requires a weakly closed 2-ideal instead of a closed one.
This notion generalizes most of the
2
-dimensional notions of exactness developed in
[
4
,
9
,
10
]
. Moreover, we show that Puppe exact 2-categories in this weaker sense still enjoy
a
2
-dimensional version of the equivalence between subobjects and quotients; hence the same is true for most of the contexts and all the examples considered in
[
4
,
9
,
10
]
, and in particular for the
2
-category of symmetric categorical groups and that of 2-dimensional vector spaces in the sense of Baez and Crans
[
2
]
. We also note that our theory expands the class of examples to include all
1
-dimensional abelian categories.

2.
2-ideals, 2-kernels, 2-cokernels

A key tool for our investigation will be a notion of a 2-dimensional ideal (of null morphisms and null 2-cells). This will be a 2-dimensional analogue of the well known concept of an ideal of null morphisms (see
[
5
]
). In this section, we propose a notion of 2-ideal, via a profunctor approach. We then explicitly characterize such a notion in terms of a class of null morphisms and a class of null 2-cells that satisfy 2-dimensional analogues of the familiar condition of closure under composition with morphisms in the category.

Moreover, we introduce 2-dimensional kernels and cokernels, with respect to a 2-ideal. In the particular case of strictly described categories enriched over pointed groupoids, our notions recover the known notions of 2-kernel and 2-cokernel (see
[
4
,
9
]
).

Remark 2.1
.

We notice that the notion of ideal (of null morphisms) in a category can be abstractly captured via a profunctor approach. Indeed, an ideal of null morphisms
\mathcal{N}
in a 1-category
\mathbb{C}
is the same as a subprofunctor
N
of the profunctor
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mkern 1.0mu}\mkern-1.5mu\left({-},{-}\right)
, or more precisely a pair
(N,\nu)
where
N\colon\mathbb{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathbb{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
is a profunctor and
\nu
is a natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Set}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mkern 1.0mu}\mkern-1.5mu\left({-},{-}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}

with injective components. Notice that the functoriality conditions together with the naturality of
\nu
give the usual conditions of closure under composition. This is similar to the common practice of viewing sieves as subfunctors of representable presheaves.

This then motivates the following definition.

Definition 2.2
.

A
2-ideal

\mathcal{N}
(of null morphisms and null 2-cells) in a 2-category
\mathfrak{L}
is a pair
(N,\nu)
where
N\colon\mathfrak{L}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathfrak{L}\to\mathbf{Cat}
is a normal pseudofunctor, i.e. a 2-dimensional profunctor, and
\nu
is an injective on objects and faithful pseudo natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{L}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathfrak{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Cat}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mkern 1.0mu}\mkern-1.5mu\left({-},{-}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}

Remark 2.3
.

In the notations of Definition
2.2
, for every
A,B\in\mathfrak{L}
, the category
N(A,B)
represents the category of null morphisms from
A
to
B
and null 2-cells between them. Pseudonaturality of
\nu
captures the following property: given a null morphism
n\colon A{\mathrel{\to}}B
and morphisms
a\colon A^{\prime}\to A
,
b\colon B\to B^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{L}
, the composite
b\circ n\circ a
is isomorphic to a null morphism. The composite itself may not be null, but there exists some replacement, isomorphic to it, which is null. And this replacement is given by
N(a,b)(n)
. This is similar to the notion of bisieve, introduced by Street in
[
11
]
. A difference from
[
11
]
is that we do not take all 2-cells to be null. Our
\nu
is indeed not full in general. This is essential to capture the expected properties of the 2-ideal
(0)
in the 2-pointed case, see Example
2.7
. Indeed, we want
N(A,B)
to be equivalent to the singleton category in the 2-pointed case, exactly as
N(A,B)
is isomorphic to the singleton set in the
1
-pointed case.

We can now characterize 2-ideals in terms of a class of null morphisms and a class of null 2-cells

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be a 2-category. A
2
-ideal
\mathcal{N}
in
\mathfrak{L}
(of null morphisms and null
2
-cells) can be equivalently given as

-

a class of morphisms, called null morphisms, and a class of 2-cells between them, called null 2-cells such that the identity 2-cell on a null morphism is a null 2-cell and null 2-cells are closed under vertical composition;

-

for every null morphism
A{\mathrel{\to}^{n}}B
and every pair
(a,b)
with
A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{a}A
and
B\xrightarrow{b}B^{\prime}
morphisms in
\mathfrak{L}
, a null morphism
A^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}^{\widetilde{b\circ n\circ a}}}B^{\prime}
and an isomorphic 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{b\circ n\circ a}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{a,b,n}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

such that

(1)

for every null morphism
A{\mathrel{\to}^{n}}B
we have
\widetilde{\operatorname{id}\circ n\circ\operatorname{id}}=n
and
\nu_{\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id},n}=\operatorname{id}
;

(2)

for every null 2-cell
\mu\colon n\Rightarrow n^{\prime}
between any null morphisms
n,n^{\prime}:A{\mathrel{\to}}B
and for every pair of morphism
(a,b)
with
A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{a}A
and
B\xrightarrow{b}B^{\prime}
the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{b\circ n\circ a}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{b\circ n^{\prime}\circ a}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 24.99316pt\nu^{-1}_{a,b,n}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 27.45152pt\nu_{a,b,n^{\prime}}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\prime}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

is a null 2-cell;

(3)

for every null morphism
A{\mathrel{\to}^{n}}B
and every pair of 2-cells
(\alpha,\beta)
with
\alpha\colon a\Rightarrow a^{\prime}\colon A^{\prime}\to A
and
\beta\colon b\Rightarrow b^{\prime}\colon B\to B^{\prime}
the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{b\circ n\circ a}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{b^{\prime}\circ n\circ a^{\prime}}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 27.45152pt\nu^{-1}_{a,b,n}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 27.45152pt\nu_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime},n}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

ia a null 2-cell;

(4)

for every null morphism
A{\mathrel{\to}^{n}}B
and every two pairs
(a,b)
,
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
with
A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{a}A
,
A^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{a^{\prime}}A^{\prime}
,
B\xrightarrow{b}B^{\prime}
,
B^{\prime}\xrightarrow{b^{\prime}}B^{\prime\prime}
morphisms in
\mathfrak{L}
the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\circ
\circ
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 38.10425pt\nu^{-1}_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime},\widetilde{b\circ n\circ a}}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 27.45152pt\nu^{-1}_{a,b,n}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 38.10425pt\nu_{a\circ a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\circ b,n}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}

is an invertible null 2-cell.

Proof.

Consider the
2
-ideal
\mathcal{N}=(N,\nu)
in
\mathfrak{L}
. For every
(A,B)\in\mathfrak{L}\times\mathfrak{L}^{\operatorname{op}}
, the normal pseudofunctor
N
gives a subcategory
N(A,B)
of the hom-category
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mkern 1.0mu}\mkern-1.5mu\left({A},{B}\right)
consisting of null morphisms and null 2-cells between them. This means that the the identity 2-cell on a null morphism is a null 2-cell and null 2-cells are closed under vertical composition. Moreover, given morphisms
A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{a}A
and
B\xrightarrow{b}B^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{L}
, the pseudonatural transformation
\mu
gives an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N(A,B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\mkern 1.0mu}\mkern-1.5mu\left({A},{B}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\mkern 1.0mu}\mkern-1.5mu\left({A^{\prime}},{B^{\prime}}\right).}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{a,b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(A,B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b\circ-\circ a}

It is then straightforward to check that the naturality of
\nu_{a,b}
corresponds to (2). Moreover, it is straightforward to prove that the pseudonaturality of
\nu
is equivalent to conditions (1), (3) and (4). In particular, condition (1) and (4) are given by the fact that the components of
\nu
respect identities and composition while condition (3) is the compatibility with 2-cells of the components of
\nu
.

Conversely, given a class of morphisms, a class of 2-cells and isomorphic null 2-cells satisfying the axioms from (1) to (4), for every
A,B\in\mathfrak{L}
we define
N(A,B)
to be the category consisting of the the morphisms from
A
to
B
that are in the chosen class of morphisms and the 2-cells between them that are in the chosen class of 2-cells. Such
N(A,B)
is a subcategory of
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mkern 1.0mu}\mkern-1.5mu\left({A},{B}\right)
, so we have an injective on objects and faithful functor
N(A,B)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mkern 1.0mu}\mkern-1.5mu\left({A},{B}\right)
. Given morphisms
A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{a}A
and
B\xrightarrow{b}B^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{L}
and a null morphism
A{\mathrel{\to}^{n}}B
, we define
N(a,b)(n)
as
\widetilde{b\circ n\circ a}
and
(\nu_{a,b})_{n}
as
\nu_{a,b,n}
. Moreover, given a 2-cell
\mu\colon n\rightarrow n^{\prime}
, we define
N(a,b)(\mu)
as the 2-cell of condition (2). And given a null morphism
A{\mathrel{\to}^{n}}B
and a pair of 2-cells
(\alpha,\beta)
with
\alpha\colon a\Rightarrow a^{\prime}\colon A^{\prime}\to A
and
\beta\colon b\Rightarrow b^{\prime}\colon B\to B^{\prime}
, we define the 2-cell
N(\alpha,\beta)_{n}
as the 2-cell of condition (3). Condition (4) then gives the isomorphisms that regulate the pseudofunctoriality of
N
. Finally, it is straightforward to prove that
\nu
is a pseudonatural transformation. So
(N,\nu)
is a 2-ideal in the sense of Definition
2.2
.
∎

We now aim at describing the 2-ideal canonically associated to a 2-pointed 2-category.

Definition 2.5
.

We call a 2-category
\mathfrak{L}

2-pointed
if
\mathfrak{L}
has a biterminal object which is also a biinitial object. We will call such object
bizero object
and denote it as
0
.

Remark 2.6
.

A bizero object
0
in a 2-category
\mathfrak{L}
is such that, for every
A\in\mathfrak{L}
both the hom-categories

{\mathfrak{L}}\left({A},\hskip 0.43057pt{0}\right)
and
{\mathfrak{L}}\left({0},\hskip 0.43057pt{A}\right)
are equivalent to the terminal category. This means that, for every
A\in\mathfrak{L}
, there exist morphisms
t\colon A\to 0
and
i\colon 0\to A
(which need not be unique), and given any two morphisms
t,t^{\prime}\colon A\to 0
(or any two morphisms
i,i^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{0}\to A
) there exists a unique isomorphic 2-cell between them.

Example 2.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be a 2-pointed 2-category, with a fixed choice
0
of a representative for the bizero object. Then
\mathfrak{L}
has a canonical 2-ideal
(0)
associated to it, given as follows. Null morphisms are precisely the ones that factor through
0
and null 2-cells are precisely the ones induced by the 2-dimensional universal property of the bizero object as shown in the following diagram

(1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B.}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{\prime}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}

Given a null morphism
n=A\xrightarrow{t}0\xrightarrow{i}B
and morphisms
A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{a}A
and
B\xrightarrow{b}B^{\prime}
, we notice that the composite
A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{a}A\xrightarrow{t}0\xrightarrow{i}B\xrightarrow{b}B^{\prime}
is null. So we define
\widetilde{b\circ n\circ a}
as such composite and
\nu_{a,b,n}
as the identity 2-cell. It is then straightforward to check that these data satisfy the required axioms (see Theorem
2.4
) using the universal properties of the bizero object.

Notice that, for every
A,B\in\mathfrak{L}
, we have that
(0)(A,B)
is equivalent to the terminal category. Indeed, the functor from the terminal category to
(0)(A,B)
that picks a fixed chosen morphism
A\xrightarrow{t}0\xrightarrow{i}B
is an equivalence of categories since given any other null morphism
A\xrightarrow{t^{\prime}}0\xrightarrow{i^{\prime}}B
the 2-cell of diagram (
1
) is an isomorphic null 2-cell.

This is a 2-dimensional analogue of the fact that
N(A,B)
is isomorphic to the singleton set in the 1-pointed case (as there is a unique null morphism
A\to 0\to B
for every
A
and
B
in the 1-pointed case).

Remark 2.8
.

There are several additional axioms that a 2-ideal of null morphisms and null 2-cells might satisfy. For example, one could consider to ask the components of
\nu
to be full, conservative, isofibrations, or discrete opfibrations.

Remark 2.9
.

The additional axioms explored in Remark
2.8
correspond to possible 2-dimensional notions of monomorphism. Indeed, the components of the pseudonatural transformation
\nu
of a 2-ideal should be 2-dimensional monomorphisms in
\mathbf{Cat}
, exactly as they are monomorphisms in
\mathbf{Set}
(i.e. injections) in dimension 1. From the point of view of elementary topos theory, discrete opfibrations give a nice notion of subobjects in dimension 2, see
[
12
]
and
[
8
]
. In many other examples, though, discrete opfibrations seem to be a bit too strong.

Following Dupont and Vitale
[
3
]
, we will consider the basic notion of monomorphism in dimension 2 given by faithful morphisms. Epimorphisms in dimension 2 will be given by cofaithful morphisms. In the terminology of
[
3
]
, these correspond to
(1,1)
-proper. Considering other notions of monomorphisms and epimorphisms in dimension 2, one would get corresponding variations of our results.

We are ready to define 2-dimensional kernels and cokernels relative to a 2-ideal.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be a 2-category and
\mathcal{N}=(N,\nu)
be a 2-ideal of null morphisms and null 2-cells in
\mathfrak{L}
. An
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernel (or just 2-kernel)
of a morphism

in

is a morphism

together with an isomorphic 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

a null morphism, that is universal in the following sense:

(1)

is isomorphic to a null morphism via an isomorphism

such that the pasting

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\gamma}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is an invertible null 2-cell;

(2)

for every morphisms

and every 2-cell

such that the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\cong

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\circ

is a null 2-cell, there exists a unique 2-cell
\mu\colon u\Rightarrow v
such that
k\star\mu=\lambda
.

Dually, we can define
\mathcal{N}
-2-cokernels
. Explicitly, the 1-dimensional universal property is exhibited by the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}

\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists u}

and analogously for the 2-dimensional universal property.

Proposition 2.11
.

\mathcal{N}
-2-kernels are faithful morphisms, i.e. 2-dimensional monomorphisms (see Remark
2.9
). Dually,
\mathcal{N}
-2-cokernels are cofaithful morphisms, i.e. 2-dimensional epimorphisms.

Proof.

Straightforward using the 2-dimensional universal property of
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernels.
∎

Lemma 2.12
.

In the notations of Definition
2.10
, if
\lambda
is an isomorphic 2-cell and
\Xi^{\lambda}
is an invertible null 2-cell, then the 2-cell
\mu
induced by the 2-dimensional universal property of the 2-kernel is an isomorphic 2-cell.

Dually for 2-cokernels.

Proof.

Applying the 2-dimensional universal property of the 2-kernel to the 2-cell
\lambda^{-1}\colon k\circ v\Rightarrow k\circ u
, we construct a 2-cell
\mu^{\prime}\colon v\Rightarrow u
such that
k\star\mu^{\prime}=\lambda^{-1}
. This implies
k\star(\mu^{\prime}\circ\mu)=\lambda^{-1}
and analogously
k\star(\mu\circ\mu^{\prime})=\lambda^{-1}
. Since
k
is faithful by Proposition
2.11
, we conclude that
\mu^{\prime}
is the inverse of
\mu
.
∎

Proposition 2.13
.

\mathcal{N}
-2-kernels and
\mathcal{N}
-2-cokernels are uniquely determined up to equivalence. More precisely, given two
\mathcal{N}
-kernels
K\xrightarrow{k}A
and
K^{\prime}\xrightarrow{k^{\prime}}A
of a morphism
f\colon A\to B
in
\mathfrak{L}
, there exist an equivalence
j\colon K\to K^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{L}
and an isomorphic 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}{{{}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

such that
(j,\gamma)
is an equivalence in the pseudo slice 2-category over
A
(i.e.
j
is an equivalence over
A
, taking into account the isomorphic 2-cell
\gamma
).

Proof.

Straightforward using the universal properties of
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernels and
\mathcal{N}
-2-cokernels.
∎

We show that in the 2-pointed case, 2-kernels are bilimits. More precisely, they are biisoinserters. This is a 2-dimensional analogue of the fact that in the 1-pointed case kernels are equalizers (of the morphism with the zero morphism).

We first recall the definition of the biisoinserter.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be a 2-category and let
f,g\colon A\to B
be two parallel morphisms in
\mathfrak{L}
. The
biisoinserter of
f
and
g
is, if it exists, an object
L\in\mathfrak{L}
together with a morphism
\ell\colon L\to A
and an isomorphic 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in
\mathfrak{L}
, which is universal in the following bicategorical sense:

(
i
)

for every morphism
m\colon M\to A
and isomorphic 2-cell
\mu\colon f\circ m\cong g\circ m
, there exist a morphism
v\colon M\to L
and an isomorphic 2-cell
\delta\colon m\cong\ell\circ v
such that

(
ii
)

for every pair of morphisms
v,w\colon M\to L
and 2-cell
\tau\colon\ell\circ v{\mathrel{\to}}\ell\circ w\colon M\to A
in
\mathfrak{L}
such that
\lambda\ast w\circ f\ast\tau=g\ast\tau\circ\lambda\ast v
, there exists a unique 2-cell
\sigma\colon v{\mathrel{\to}}w
such that
\ell\ast\sigma=\tau
.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be a 2-pointed 2-category, with a fixed choice
0
of a representative for the bizero object. Consider the 2-ideal
(0)
canonically associated to
\mathfrak{L}
. Then the 2-kernel of a morphism
f\colon A\to B
is given by the biisoinserter of
f\colon A\to B
and a chosen null morphism
A\to 0\to B
(one always exists because
0
is a bizero object).

As a consequence,
2
-kernels are faithful and conservative in the 2-pointed case.

Dually for 2-cokernels.

Proof.

We prove that the structure 2-cell of the biisoinserter of
f
and the chosen
A\xrightarrow{t}0\xrightarrow{i}B
gives the structure isomorphic 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

of a 2-kernel of
f
. Notice that indeed the composite morphism below is null.

Given a morphism
M\xrightarrow{m}A
and an isomorphic 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

we can paste
\beta^{-1}
with the unique isomorphic 2-cells
a\cong t\circ m
and
b\cong i
to obtain an isomorphic 2-cell
f\circ m\cong i\circ t\circ m
, and thus induce
v\colon M\to L
and an isomorphic 2-cell
\delta:m\cong\ell\circ v
by the universal property of the biisoinserter. It is straightforward to show that
v
and
\delta
satisfy the required axiom for the 1-dimensional universal property of the 2-kernel.

Consider then
v,w\colon M\to K
and a 2-cell
\tau\colon\ell\circ v{\mathrel{\to}}\ell\circ w
such that
f\ast\tau
, pasted with
\lambda
and
\lambda^{-1}
, gives a null 2-cell, which means that it factors through
i
. The latter assumption guarantees that we can apply the 2-dimensional universal property of the biisoinserter to the 2-cell
\tau
. And we thus obtain that there exists a unique 2-cell
\sigma\colon v{\mathrel{\to}}w
such that
\ell\ast\sigma=\tau
.
∎

Remark 2.16
.

Biisoinserters are a 2-dimensional analogue of equalizers. Indeed, they equalize parallel morphisms up to an isomorphic 2-cell, in a 2-universal way.

Remark 2.17
.

It is straightforward to see that in the particular case of (strictly described) categories enriched over pointed groupoids, the 2-pointed version of our notions recovers the known notions of 2-kernel and 2-cokernel in the literature (see
[
4
,
9
]
). Our notions are much more general, as they do not require to have a bizero object nor all 2-cells to be isomorphisms.

The notion of Grandis exact category involves closed ideals of null morphisms. We thus need a 2-dimensional analogue of closedness for an ideal.

Recall that the following well known result holds in dimension 1.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a category and
\mathcal{N}
be an ideal of null morphisms in
\mathbb{C}
. Assume that
\mathbb{C}
has all kernels and cokernels. The following conditions are equivalent:

(
i
)

\mathcal{N}
is closed, i.e. every null morphism factors through an object whose identity is a null morphism (called null object);

(
ii
)

All kernels reflect null morphisms, i.e. if
k\circ f
is a null morphism with
k
a kernel then
f
is a null morphism.

(
iii
)

All cokernels coreflect null morphisms, i.e. if
f\circ c
is a null morphism with
c
a cokernel then
f
is a null morphism.

Conditions
(ii)
and
(iii)
of Proposition
2.18
are easier and more straightforward to generalize to dimension 2.

Definition 2.19
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be a 2-category and
\mathcal{N}=(N,\nu)
be a 2-ideal of null morphisms and null 2-cells in
\mathfrak{L}
. We say that a morphism
K\xrightarrow{k}A

reflects null morphisms
if whenever a morphism
D\xrightarrow{s}K
is such that
D\xrightarrow{s}K\xrightarrow{k}A
is isomorphic to a null morphism
D{\mathrel{\to}^{n}}A
via a 2-cell
\delta\colon k\circ s\Rightarrow n
, there exist a null morphism
D{\mathrel{\to}^{\widehat{\psi}}}K
and an isomorphic 2-cell
\psi\colon s\Rightarrow\hat{\psi}
such that
\delta
coincides with

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\psi}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 6.02777pt\nu}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

up to an invertible null 2-cell
\xi\colon m\Rightarrow n
. Equivalently, the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\circ
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\psi}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 6.02777pt\nu}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

is an invertible null 2-cell.

We say that
K\xrightarrow{k}A

reflects null 2-cells
if
whenever a 2-cell
\mu\colon s{\mathrel{\to}}s^{\prime}\colon D\to K
between null morphisms
s
and
s^{\prime}
is such that the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\circ
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

is a null 2-cell, we have that the 2-cell
\mu
is a null 2-cell.

Dually for morphisms coreflecting null morphisms and null 2-cells.

Definition 2.20
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be a 2-category and
\mathcal{N}=(N,\nu)
be a 2-ideal of null morphisms and null 2-cells in
\mathfrak{L}
. Assume that
\mathfrak{L}
has all
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernels and
\mathcal{N}
-2-cokernels. We call
\mathcal{N}
a
closed 2-ideal
if all
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernels reflect null morphisms and null 2-cells and all
\mathcal{N}
-2-cokernels coreflect null morphisms and null 2-cells.

We show that a generalization to dimension 2 of condition
(i)
of Proposition
2.18
is equivalent to having a weaker version of a closed 2-ideal.

Definition 2.21
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be a 2-category and
\mathcal{N}=(N,\nu)
be a 2-ideal of null morphisms and null 2-cells in
\mathfrak{L}
. We say that an
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernel
K\xrightarrow{k}A
, say of
f\colon A\to B
with structure 2-cell
\alpha
,
weakly reflects null morphisms
if whenever a morphism
D\xrightarrow{s}K
is such that
D\xrightarrow{s}K\xrightarrow{k}A
is isomorphic to a null morphism
D{\mathrel{\to}^{n}}A
via a 2-cell
\delta\colon k\circ s\Rightarrow n
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\circ
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{-1}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\circ
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\circ
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is an invertible null 2-cell, there exist a null morphism
D{\mathrel{\to}^{\widehat{\psi}}}K
and an isomorphic 2-cell
\psi\colon s\Rightarrow\hat{\psi}
such that the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\circ
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\psi}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 6.02777pt\nu}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

is an invertible null 2-cell.

Dually for
\mathcal{N}
-2-cokernels weakly coreflecting null morphisms.

Remark 2.22
.

Notice that Definition
2.21
is a weaker version of Definition
2.19
, containing the extra assumption that
\delta
is compatible with the way in which
k
is a 2-kernel.

Definition 2.23
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be a 2-category and
\mathcal{N}
be a 2-ideal of null morphisms and null 2-cells in
\mathfrak{L}
. An object
Z
of
\mathfrak{L}
is called a
null object
if the identity of
Z
is isomorphic to a null morphism
\widehat{\xi^{Z}}\colon Z{\mathrel{\to}}Z
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\xi^{Z}}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\xi^{Z}}

Proposition 2.24
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be a 2-category and
\mathcal{N}=(N,\nu)
be a 2-ideal of null morphisms and null 2-cells in
\mathfrak{L}
. Assume that
\mathfrak{L}
has all
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernels. The following conditions are equivalent:

(
i
)

All
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernels weakly reflect null morphisms;

(
ii
)

Every morphism
h\colon A\to B
isomorphic to a null morphism
n
, via an isomorphic 2-cell
\rho
, factors up to an isomorphic 2-cell
\gamma
through a null object
Z
, making

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\circ
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\xi^{Z}}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{Z}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

into an invertible null 2-cell, where
\xi^{Z}
is the isomorphic 2-cell associated to the null object
Z
.

(
iii
)

All
\mathcal{N}
-2-cokernels weakly coreflect null morphisms;

Proof.

We prove
(i){\mathrel{\to}}(ii)
. Let
h\colon A\to B
be a morphism in
\mathfrak{L}
isomorphic to a null morphism
n
via a 2-cell
\rho
. Consider then the
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernel
Z\xrightarrow{k}{B}
of the identity of
B
, and call
\alpha
its structure 2-cell. Then by the universal property of the
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernel, there exist
u\colon A\to Z
and an isomorphic 2-cell
\gamma\colon h{\mathrel{\to}}k\circ u
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\gamma}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{-1}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\cong

is an invertible null 2-cell. We show that
Z
is a null object. The identity
Z{\mathrel{\to}}Z
is such that
k\circ\operatorname{id}_{Z}
is isomorphic to a null morphism, via
\alpha
, making

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\circ
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\circ
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\circ
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

into the identity 2-cell and thus into an invertible null 2-cell. Since
k
weakly reflects null morphisms, there exist a null morphism
\widehat{\xi^{Z}}\colon Z{\mathrel{\to}}Z
and an isomorphic 2-cell
\xi^{Z}\colon\operatorname{id}_{Z}{\mathrel{\to}}\widehat{\xi}
such that the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
\circ
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\xi^{Z}}}
\circ
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{Z}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 6.02777pt\nu}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

is an invertible null 2-cell. We conclude that
Z
is a null object and that
(ii)
holds.

We now prove
(ii){\mathrel{\to}}(i)
. Let
f\colon A\to B
be a morphism in
\mathfrak{L}
and
K\xrightarrow{k}A
be the
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernel of
f
, with structure 2-cell
\alpha
. Consider then
D\xrightarrow{s}K
and an isomorphic 2-cell
\delta\colon k\circ s{\mathrel{\to}}n
with
n
a null morphism, such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\circ
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{-1}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\circ
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\circ
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is an invertible null 2-cell. By
(ii)
, since
n
is a null morphism, there exist a null object
Z
and an isomorphic 2-cell
\gamma
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\circ
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\xi^{Z}}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{Z}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

is an invertible null 2-cell. By the universal property of the
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernel, there exist
u\colon Z\to K
and an isomorphic 2-cell
\lambda\colon y{\mathrel{\to}}k\circ u
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z}}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi^{Z}}^{-1}}
\cong
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\xi^{Z}}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists u}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\lambda}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is an invertible null 2-cell. It is then straightforward to see that we can apply the 2-dimensional universal property of
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernel to the isomorphic 2-cell
\Theta
given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
.
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

We obtain that there exists a unique isomorphic 2-cell
\mu\colon s{\mathrel{\to}}u\circ x
such that
k\ast\mu=\Theta
. So
s
is isomorphic to a null morphism via

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\circ
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\xi^{Z}}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

It is now straightforward to conclude.

We have thus proved that
(i)
and
(ii)
are equivalent. Dually, we obtain that also
(ii)
and
(iii)
are equivalent.
∎

In the last part of this section, we introduce a notion of equivalence between 2-ideals. We will show that equivalent 2-ideals have same 2-kernels and 2-cokernels. This will be an important ingredient for the proof of Theorem
3.4
.

Definition 2.25
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be a 2-category and let
\mathcal{N}=(N,\nu)
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}=(N^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
be 2-ideals of null morphisms and null 2-cells in
\mathfrak{L}
. We say that
\mathcal{N}
is

to

if there exist a pseudonatural equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{L}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathfrak{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Cat}}}

We aim at proving some useful explicit characterizations of equivalence of 2-ideals.

Definition 2.26
.

Let
\mathcal{N}=(N,\nu)
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}=(N^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
be 2-ideals in a 2-category
\mathfrak{L}
and consider the following conditions:

(A)

for every
A{\mathrel{\to}^{n}}B
in
\mathcal{N}
there exist
A{\mathrel{\to}^{n^{\prime}}}B
in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
and an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B;}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\prime}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{A,B,n}}

(B)

for every
A{\mathrel{\to}^{n^{\prime}}}B
in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
there exist
A{\mathrel{\to}^{m}}B
in
\mathcal{N}
and an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\prime}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi^{\prime}_{A,B,n^{\prime}}}

Assuming that (A) and (B) are satisfied, consider the following properties relative to the isomorphisms
\Xi
and
\Xi^{\prime}
of properties (A) and (B):

(1)

for every null 2-cell
\alpha\colon m{\mathrel{\to}}n\colon A\to B
in
\mathcal{N}
the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{A,B,m}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi^{-1}_{A,B,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\prime}}
\circ

is a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
;

(1’)

for every null 2-cell
\beta\colon n^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}s^{\prime}\colon A\to B
in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi^{\prime}_{A,B,n^{\prime}}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi^{\prime-1}_{A,B,s^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\prime}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\circ

is a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}
;

(2)

for every null morphisms
m,n\colon A{\mathrel{\to}}B
in
\mathcal{N}
and every null 2-cell
\beta\colon m^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}n^{\prime}\colon A\to B
in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
(where
m^{\prime}
and
n^{\prime}
are the morphisms in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
associated to
m
and
n
) the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi^{-1}_{A,B,m}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{A,B,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\prime}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\circ

is a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}
;

(1+2)

for every 2-cell
\alpha\colon m{\mathrel{\to}}n\colon A\to B
between null morphisms
m
and
n
in
\mathcal{N}
the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{A,B,m}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi^{-1}_{A,B,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\prime}}
\circ

is a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
if and only if
\alpha
is a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}
;

(3)

for every pair of morphisms
(a,b)\colon(A,B)\to(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime};}
\circ
\circ
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu^{\prime}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi^{-1}_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime},\widehat{n}}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\prime}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{A,B,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

is an invertible null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
;

(4)

for every null morphism
A{\mathrel{\to}^{n^{\prime}}}B
in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\circ
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Xi^{\prime}}^{-1}_{A,B,n^{\prime}}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi^{-1}_{A,B,m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\prime}}
\circ

is an invertible null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
;

(4’)

for every null morphism
A{\mathrel{\to}^{m}}B
in
\mathcal{N}
the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\circ
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Xi}^{-1}_{A,B,m}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Xi^{\prime}}^{-1}_{A,B,m^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\circ

is an invertible null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}
.

Theorem 2.27
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be a 2-category and let
\mathcal{N}=(N,\nu)
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}=(N^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
be 2-ideals of null morphisms and null 2-cells in
\mathfrak{L}
. The following facts are equivalent:

(i)

\mathcal{N}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
;

(ii)

conditions (A) and (B) hold and the isomorphisms
\Xi
and
\Xi^{\prime}
satisfy properties (1), (2), (3) and (4);

(iii)

conditions (A) and (B) hold and the isomorphisms
\Xi
and
\Xi^{\prime}
satisfy properties (1+ 2), (3) and (4);

(iv)

conditions (A) and (B) hold and the isomorphisms
\Xi
and
\Xi^{\prime}
satisfy properties (1), (1’), (3), (4) and (4’).

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii). Condition (A) holds thanks to the existence of the invertible modification
\Xi
and the isomorphisms
\Xi^{\prime}
of condition (B) are constructed by pasting the isomorphic diagrams related by
\Xi
(as in Definition
2.25
) with the pseudoinverse of the pseudonatural equivalence
\iota
. The 2-cell of property (1) is precisely the
\iota_{A,B}(\alpha)
and so it is a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
. Moreover, property (2) is precisely the fullness of the equivalence of categories
\iota_{A,B}
. The 2-cell of property (3) is then precisely given by the action of structure 2-cell
\iota_{(a,b)}
on the null morphism
A{\mathrel{\to}^{n}}B
in
\mathcal{N}
and thus it is a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
. Finally, (4) is precisely given by the essential surjectivity of the equivalence of categories
\iota_{A,B}
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i). (A) allows us to define a function
\iota_{A,B}:N(A,B)\to N^{\prime}(A,B)
, that then extends to a functor thanks to (A) and (1).
\iota_{A,B}
is automatically faithful, it full thanks to (2) and it is essentially surjective thanks to (B) and (4). So
\iota_{A,B}
is an equivalence of categories for every
a,b\in\mathfrak{L}
. Property (3) then allows us to define the structure 2-cells of
\iota
and these are automatically natural transformations. It is then straightforward to see that
\iota
constructed this way is automatically a pseudonatural transformation. It remains to define the invertible modification
\Xi
. Property (A) allows us to define the invertible components
\Xi_{A,B,m}
that form a natural transformation
\Xi_{A,B}
by construction of the action of
\iota_{A,B}
on morphisms. Finally,
\Xi
satisfies the axiom of modification by construction of the structure 2-cells of
\iota
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii). We show that properties (1) and (2) imply property (1+2). Let
\alpha\colon m{\mathrel{\to}}n\colon A\to B
be a 2-cell between null morphisms
m
and
n
in
\mathcal{N}
. If
\alpha
is a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}
then clearly the 2-cell of property (1+2) is a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
by property (1). Conversely, if such 2-cell is null in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
then applying property (2) to it we precisely conclude that
\alpha
is a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}
.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii). Property (1+2) clearly implies (1). Moreover, given null morphisms
m,n\colon A{\mathrel{\to}}B
in
\mathcal{N}
and a null 2-cell
\beta\colon m^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}n^{\prime}\colon A\to B
in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
(where
m^{\prime}
and
n^{\prime}
are the morphisms in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
associated to
m
and
n
), then the 2-cell of property (2) is such that its pasting with
\Xi
and
\Xi^{-1}
gives the null 2-cell
\beta
in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
and so it is a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}
by (1+2) .

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(iv). We show that (1+2) together with (4) implies both (1’) and (4’). Let
\beta\colon n^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}s^{\prime}\colon A\to B
be a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
. Thanks to (1+2), to prove that (1’) holds it suffices to show that the 2-cell of (1’) is a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
when pasted with
\Xi_{A,B,m}
and
\Xi^{-1}_{A,B,r}
. And this is true because the obtained 2-cell is the pasting of the null 2-cell
\beta
in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
with the invertible null 2-cells in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
given by (4) applied to
m
and
r
. So (1’) holds. Let now
A{\mathrel{\to}^{m}}B
be a null morphism in
\mathcal{N}
. Thanks to (1+2), is suffices to show that the 2-cell of (4’) is an invertible null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
once pasted with
\Xi_{A,B,m}
and
\Xi^{-1}_{A,B,l}
. But this is guaranteed by (4) and so (4’) holds.

(iv)
\Rightarrow
(ii). We prove that (1’) and (4’) imply (2). Let
m,n\colon A{\mathrel{\to}}B
be null morphisms in
\mathcal{N}
and let
\beta\colon m^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}n^{\prime}\colon A\to B
be a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
(where
m^{\prime}
and
n^{\prime}
are the morphisms in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
associated to
m
and
n
). Thanks to (1’) the 2-cell
\beta
pasted with
{\Xi^{\prime}}^{-1}_{A,B,m^{\prime}}
and
{\Xi^{\prime}}^{-1}_{A,B,n^{\prime}}
gives a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}
. Pasting this null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}
with the invertible null 2-cells given by property (4’) for
m^{\prime}
and
n^{\prime}
we obtain the 2-cell involved in property (2) and thus we conclude that it is a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}
.
∎

We now prove that equivalent 2-ideals have same 2-kernels and 2-cokernels.

Theorem 2.28
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be a 2-category and let
\mathcal{N}=(N,\nu)
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}=(N^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
be equivalent 2-ideals of null morphisms and null 2-cells in
\mathfrak{L}
, via a pseudonatural equivalence
\iota
and an invertible modification
\Xi
. Then
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
have same 2-kernels and 2-cokernels.

More precisely, if
k\colon K\to A
is the
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernel of a morphism
f\colon A\to B
in
\mathfrak{L}
with structure 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

then
k
is also the
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
-2-kernel of
f
, with structure 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\prime}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi^{-1}_{K,B,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Proof.

Let
l\colon L\to A
be a morphism such that
f\circ l
is isomorphic to a null morphism
s^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
, via an isomorphic 2-cell
\beta
. Pasting with
\Xi^{\prime}_{L,B,s^{\prime}}
, we obtain that
f\circ l
is also isomorphic to a null morphism
r
in
\mathcal{N}
. Then, by the universal property of the
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernel, there exist a morphism
L\xrightarrow{u}K
and an isomorphic 2-cell
\gamma\colon l{\mathrel{\to}}k\circ u
such that the pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\circ
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi^{\prime}_{L,B,s^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\gamma}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is an invertible null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}
. Since
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
are equivalent 2-ideals, by Theorem
2.27
properties (1), (2), (3) and (4) of all hold. Applying property (1) to the invertible null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}
above and pasting with the invertible null 2-cells of properties (4) and (3), we obtain that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\circ
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\circ
\circ
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi^{-1}_{K,B,n}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is an invertible null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
. So that the 1-dimensional universal property of
k
being the
N^{\prime}
-2-kernel of
f
holds.

Consider now morphisms
u,v\colon L\to K
and a 2-cell
\lambda\colon k\circ u{\mathrel{\to}}k\circ v
such that
f\ast\lambda
, pasted with
\alpha,\alpha^{-1},\Xi^{-1}_{K,B,n},\Xi_{K,B,n}
and copies of
\nu^{\prime}
, gives a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
. Applying properties (3) and (2) of Definition listofpropertieslistofproperties, it is straightforward to show that
f\ast\lambda
also gives, when pasted with
\alpha,\alpha^{-1}
and copies of
\nu
, a null 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}
. Then, by the 2-dimensional universal property of the
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernel, there exists a unique 2-cell
\mu\colon u{\mathrel{\to}}v
such that
k\ast\mu=\lambda
. So that the 2-dimensional universal property of the
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
-2-kernel holds.
∎

3.
Grandis exact 2-categories

It is shown in
[
7
]
that Grandis exact categories can be equivalently characterized as categories equipped with a proper factorization system such that the opfibration of subobjects relative to the factorization system is isomorphic to the fibration of relative quotients. In this section, we develop a 2-dimensional analogue of this fibrational approach to exactness. As an outcome, we propose a 2-dimensional notion of Grandis exact category. In particular we obtain a 2-dimensional notion of Puppe exact category, in the pointed case, which we will compare in the next section with existing similar notions developed by Dupont and Nakaoka.

Remark 3.1
.

We will need the notion of factorization system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
on a 2-category, studied by Dupont and Vitale in
[
3
]
. We will also use their notion of
(1,1)
-proper factorization system on a 2-category. This means that every morphism in
\mathcal{E}
is cofaithful and every morphism in
\mathcal{M}
is faithful.

We will then need the following notion of 2-dimensional fibration, which is a weaker version of the bicategorical fibrations studied by Baković in
[
1
]
. More precisely, we will only ask weak 2-fibrations to be locally an isofibration rather than a fibration.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
P\colon\mathfrak{K}\to{\mathfrak{L}}
be a 2-functor. We call
P
a
weak 2-fibration
if it satisfies the following conditions:

-

for every
E\in\mathfrak{K}
and every morphism
f\colon L\to P(E)
in
\mathfrak{L}
, there exists a 2-cartesian lifting of
f
to
E
, in the bicategorical sense described by Baković in
[
1
, Definition 4.6]
;

-

P
is locally an isofibration, i.e. for every
E,E^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}
the induced functor
{\mathfrak{K}}\left({E},\hskip 0.43057pt{E^{\prime}}\right)\to{\mathfrak{L}}\left({P(E)},\hskip 0.43057pt{P(E^{\prime})}\right)
between hom-categories is an isofibration.

Dually, we can define
weak 2-opfibrations
.

We generalize to dimension 2 the important fact that subobjects relative to a factorization system form an opfibration and quotients relative to a factorization system form a fibration. For this, we will need the notion of pseudo arrow category of a 2-category
\mathfrak{L}
, whose objects are arrows in
\mathfrak{L}
, morphisms are squares in
\mathfrak{L}
filled with an isomorphism and 2-cells are pairs of two 2-cells in
\mathfrak{L}
coherent with the isomorphisms filling the squares.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
be a factorization system on a 2-category
\mathfrak{L}
. Consider
\mathfrak{E}
and
\mathfrak{M}
the full sub-2-categories of the pseudo arrow category of
\mathfrak{L}
on morphisms in
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{M}
respectively. Then the domain 2-functor
\operatorname{dom}\colon\mathfrak{E}\to\mathfrak{L}
is a weak 2-fibration, called the weak 2-fibration of 2-quotients relative to
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
.

Dually, the codomain 2-functor
\operatorname{cod}\colon\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{L}
is a weak 2-opfibration, called the weak 2-opfibration of 2-subobjects relative to
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
.

Proof.

We prove that
\operatorname{cod}\colon\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{L}
is a weak-2-opfibration. Then
\operatorname{dom}\colon\mathfrak{E}\to\mathfrak{L}
will be a weak 2-fibration, dually. Of course
\operatorname{cod}
is a 2-functor, being a restriction of the codomain 2-functor from the pseudo arrow category. Consider
m\colon A\rightarrowtail B
in
\mathcal{M}
and a morphism
f\colon B\to C
in
\mathfrak{L}
, we construct the 2-cocartesian lifting of
f
to
m
using the factorization of
f\circ m
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{f\circ m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{f\circ m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f\circ m}^{-1}}

We show that such morphism in
\mathfrak{M}
is indeed 2-cocartesian. So consider
(z,h,\varphi)\colon m\to n
in
\mathfrak{M}
with
n\colon X\rightarrowtail Y
, a morphism
g\colon C\to Y
in
\mathfrak{L}
and an isomorphic 2-cell
\xi\colon h\to g\circ f
in
\mathfrak{L}
. Then the diagonal lifting property of the factorization system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
induces a fill-in for the square on the left below:

(\alpha,\xi)
is an isomorphic 2-cell in
\mathfrak{M}
between
\beta\circ\theta_{f\circ m}^{-1}
and
\varphi
.

\mathfrak{M}

Consider then also
d,\alpha,\beta
associated to
\varphi,g,\xi
as above, and
d^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}
analogously associated to
\varphi^{\prime},g^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}
. We need to show that there exists a unique 2-cell
(\lambda,\rho)
in
\mathfrak{M}
from
(d,g,\beta)
to
(d^{\prime},g^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
such that

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Notice that
(\sigma,\rho\ast r^{f\circ m})
is a 2-cell from the square formed by
\alpha
and
\beta
to the square formed by
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime}
. By the 2-dimensional diagonal lifting property, there exists a unique 2-cell
\lambda\colon d{\mathrel{\to}}d^{\prime}
that works. We conclude that
(\ell^{f\circ m},f,\theta_{f\circ m}^{-1})
is a 2-cartesian lifting of
f
to
m
.

It remains to prove that
\operatorname{cod}\colon\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{L}
is locally an isofibration. So consider a morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

and an isomorphic 2-cell
\beta\colon b{\mathrel{\to}}b^{\prime}\colon B\to B^{\prime}
. It is straightforward to show that we can lift
\beta
to an isomorphic 2-cell in
\mathfrak{M}
with domain
(a,b,\varphi)
.
∎

We are now ready to prove the main theorem of this section, which provides a fibrational approach to define
Grandis exact 2-categories
.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be a 2-category. The following conditions are equivalent:

(
i
)

There exists a
(1,1)
-proper factorization system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
on the 2-category
\mathfrak{L}
and a biequivalence over
\mathfrak{L}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{dom}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\simeq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{cod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}

between the weak 2-fibration of 2-quotients relative to
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
and the weak 2-opfibration of 2-subobjects relative to
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
, with
K,C
normal pseudofunctors.

(
ii
)

\mathfrak{L}
has a 2-ideal
\mathcal{N}
of null morphisms and null 2-cells such that

-

\mathfrak{L}
has all
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernels and
\mathcal{N}
-2-cokernels;

-

\mathcal{N}
is a closed 2-ideal;

-

every
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernel
m
is an
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernel of its
\mathcal{N}
-2-cokernel
c_{m}
, with structure isomorphic 2-cell between
c_{m}\circ m
and a null morphism given up to an invertible null 2-cell by the
\mathcal{N}
-2-cokernel
c_{m}
of
m
; and dually for
\mathcal{N}
-2-cokernels;

-

every morphism
f
in
\mathfrak{L}
factorizes up to isomorphism as an
\mathcal{N}
-2-cokernel followed by an
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernel

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2-\operatorname{coker}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2-\operatorname{ker}}

Proof.

We prove
(i){\mathrel{\to}}(ii)
. We define a 2-ideal
\mathcal{N}
of null morphisms and null 2-cells in
\mathfrak{L}
as follows. Null morphisms are precisely those morphisms
n\colon A\to B
that factor through
K(\operatorname{id}_{B})
, which we denote as
K(\operatorname{id}_{B})\xrightarrow{k_{\operatorname{id}_{B}}}B
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\operatorname{id}_{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\operatorname{id}_{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\overline{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}

Null 2-cells between null morphisms
n=k_{\operatorname{id}_{B}}\circ\overline{n}\colon A{\mathrel{\to}}B
and
n^{\prime}=k_{\operatorname{id}_{B}}\circ\overline{n^{\prime}}\colon A{\mathrel{\to}}B
are precisely those 2-cells
\mu
that factor through
K(\operatorname{id}_{B})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\operatorname{id}_{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\operatorname{id}_{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\overline{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{n^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\prime}}

Notice that
\overline{\mu}
is unique because
K(\operatorname{id}_{B})\in\mathcal{M}
is faithful, as
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
is
(1,1)
-proper. The idea behind this construction is that we call null those morphisms that make the largest subobject of
A
, which is
\operatorname{id}_{A}
, factor through the “smallest subobject” of
B
, which is given by the image through
K
of the smallest quotient of
B
which is
\operatorname{id}_{B}
. We will show below that the 2-ideal constructed dually using
C(\operatorname{id}_{A})
is equivalent to the one above, so that 2-kernels and 2-cokernels are the same for the two 2-ideals.

We show that
\mathcal{N}
is a 2-ideal of null morphisms and null 2-cells in
\mathfrak{L}
. Given
A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{a}A
,
B\xrightarrow{b}B^{\prime}
and a null morphism
A{\mathrel{\to}^{n}}B
, we construct the null replacement of
b\circ n\circ a
with the following pasting:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\operatorname{id}_{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\operatorname{id}_{B^{\prime}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{K(\operatorname{id}^{b})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\operatorname{id}_{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\operatorname{id}_{B^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\overline{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\operatorname{id}^{b})}

where
(\widehat{K(\operatorname{id}^{b})},b,{K(\operatorname{id}^{b})})
is given by applying
K
to the morphism
\operatorname{id}^{b}:=(b,b,\operatorname{id})
in
\mathfrak{E}
. It is straightforward to prove that the axioms of 2-ideal listed in Theorem
2.4
are satisfied. We thus have a 2-ideal
\mathcal{N}=(N,\nu)
in
\mathfrak{L}
.

We show that the 2-ideal
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
constructed dually to
\mathcal{N}
using
C(\operatorname{id}_{A})
rather than
K(\operatorname{id}_{B})
is equivalent to the one above (in the sense of Definition
2.25
). Let
s^{\prime}\colon A\to B
be a null morphism in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
, i.e. a morphism
s^{\prime}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(\operatorname{id}_{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\operatorname{id}_{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\widetilde{s^{\prime}}}

We can apply
K
to the identity 2-cell
\operatorname{id}^{s^{\prime}}
above, seen as a morphism in
\mathfrak{E}
, and paste with the unit
\eta
of the biadjunction to obtain the isomorphic 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(c_{\operatorname{id}_{A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\operatorname{id}_{B})}
.
.
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\eta_{\operatorname{id}_{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{c_{\operatorname{id}_{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{K(\operatorname{id}^{s^{\prime}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\operatorname{id}_{B}}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\operatorname{id}_{A}}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\operatorname{id}^{s^{\prime}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}

Such 2-cell is an isomorphic 2-cell
\Xi_{A,B,s^{\prime}}
from a null morphism in
\mathcal{N}
to
s^{\prime}
, so that condition (A) of Definition
2.26
is satisfied. Dually, given a null morphism
n
in
\mathcal{N}
, we can use
C
and the counit
\epsilon
of the biadjunction to construct an isomorphic 2-cell
\Xi^{\prime}_{A,B,n}
from a null morphism in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
to
n
, so that also condition (B) is satisfied. In order to show that property (1) of Definition
2.26
holds, we can view a null 2-cell
\tau\colon l^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}s^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
, i.e. a 2-cell that factors through
c_{\operatorname{id}_{A}}
, as a 2-cell in
\mathfrak{E}
from
\operatorname{id}^{l^{\prime}}
to
\operatorname{id}^{s^{\prime}}
(where
\operatorname{id}^{l^{\prime}}
is analogous to
\operatorname{id}_{s^{\prime}}
, drawn above, for
l^{\prime}
in place of
s^{\prime}
). Applying
K
to such 2-cell in
\mathfrak{E}
, we get a 2-cell in
\mathfrak{M}
with second component
\tau
, which shows that property (1) holds. Dually, we get that also (1’) holds. It is then straightforward to prove properties (3) and (4), whence also (4’) will hold as it is dual to (4), using similar arguments to the one above. The strategy is always to find a good 2-cell in
\mathfrak{E}
and apply
K
to it. Thanks to Theorem
2.27
, we conclude that the 2-ideals
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
are equivalent. Whence 2-kernels and 2-cokernels are the same for the two 2-ideals, by Theorem
2.28
. This fact will be useful later in this proof.

Consider now
f\colon A\to B
in
\mathfrak{L}
. Since
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
is a factorization system on the 2-category
\mathfrak{L}
,
f
factorizes up to an isomorphic 2-cell
\theta_{f}
as
m\circ e
with
e\in\mathcal{E}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
. We prove that the morphism
K(e)\in\mathcal{M}
, denoted as
K(e)\xrightarrow{k_{e}}A
, is an
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernel of
f
. Notice that
\theta_{f}
gives the following morphism in
\mathcal{E}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}

Then applying
K
to it we obtain the structure isomorphic 2-cell for the would be
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernel
k_{e}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\operatorname{id}_{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\theta_{f})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{K(\theta_{f})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\operatorname{id}_{B}}}

We first show the 1-dimensional part of the universal property of the
\mathcal{N}
-2-kernel. So consider
z\colon Z\to A
and an isomorphic 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\operatorname{id}_{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\operatorname{id}_{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

We need to construct a morphism
u\colon Z\to K(e)
and an isomorphic 2-cell
\gamma\colon z\to k_{e}\circ u
, which we can view as a morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{-1}}

in
\mathfrak{M}
. The biequivalence over
\mathfrak{L}
gives an equivalence of categories

{\mathfrak{M}}\left({\operatorname{id}_{z}},\hskip 0.43057pt{k_{e}}\right)\simeq{\mathfrak{E}}\left({c_{\operatorname{id}_{z}}},\hskip 0.43057pt{e}\right)

over
{\mathfrak{L}}\left({Z},\hskip 0.43057pt{A}\right)
. So it suffices to construct a morphism
c_{\operatorname{id}_{z}}\to e
in
\mathfrak{E}
. By the diagonal lifting property of the factorization system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
on
\mathfrak{L}
, we obtain a fill-in for the isomorphic 2-cell on the left below:

\hbox to174.76pt{\vbox to78.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 87.38072pt\lower-40.54448pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
